- §6 Conclusion starts at the bottom

But looking carefully at the bottom of page 8, I can see "6 Conclusion" and the beginning of the conclusion paragraph. Then looking at page 9, I see at the very top: "future LLM evaluation frameworks should model" and "these facets jointly." - that's still about 2 lines of Conclusion overflowing to page 9!


Then "Code and data will be released upon acceptance at [anonymized URL]." also on page 9 before Limitations.

So the Contamination Avoidance compression wasn't enough. I need to compress more. Let me look at what else I can shorten in Related Work or §4.2-4.3.

I think the Judge Bias paragraph in Related Work is a good target. I can trim it down significantly - the key point is that CyclicJudge addresses average bias through round-robin assignment but doesn't fully handle residual variation. I'll compress that to be much tighter.

Compressing the Judge Bias paragraph... I'm looking at how to compress the related work section further. The Fiedler and Ye/Panickssery citations can be merged since they're both documenting judge biases without modeling contamination. I need to find about 3 lines of savings total to fit in the Conclusion and data availability statement, so I'm also examining the IRT paragraph for additional compression opportunities. I'm looking at how to tighten the contamination detection section—methods like ConStat, DCR, LogProber, and Min-K% all detect benchmark contamination but assume fixed raters, so judge severity effects can get mistaken for contamination. The preference leakage work identifies judge-side contamination as a complementary angle to what I'm focusing on with examinee-side effects. I'm realizing the Contamination Avoidance compression only freed up about 1.5 lines, but the addition of the |Δ/SE| = 6.4 metric in the experiments section added text back. I need to compress the Judge Bias paragraph further to reclaim another 1-2 lines and push that final conclusion sentence onto the next page.

UDA \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor adjustment reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\%, though the correction is a black box with no contamination mechanism. \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability through $J/\\Delta J$ diagnostics, identifying sign-reversal failure modes. \\citet{ye2024justice} and \\citet{panickssery2024selffavor} further document position, verbosity, and self-favoritism biases. All model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination into abilities (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).",
    "newString": "CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias via round-robin assignment but treats residual variation as genuine ability. UDA \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\% without modeling contamination. \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability via $J/\\Delta J$ diagnostics; \\citet{ye2024justice} and \\citet{panickssery2024selffavor} document position, verbosity, and self-favoritism biases. All model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination into abilities (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related_work}\n\n\\paragraph{Judge Bias in LLM Evaluation.}\nCyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026} eliminates average bias through round-robin judge assignment but treats residual score variation as genuine ability. UDA \\citep{uda2026} learns a neural K-factor adjustment reducing inter-judge deviation by 63.4\\%, though the correction is a black box with no contamination mechanism. \\citet{fiedler2026bias_uncertainty} formalize judge variability through $J/\\Delta J$ diagnostics, identifying sign-reversal failure modes. \\citet{ye2024justice} and \\citet{panickssery2024selffavor} further document position, verbosity, and self-favoritism biases. All model only the judge facet, absorbing contamination into abilities (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Detection and Adjustment.}\nSeveral methods detect or adjust for benchmark contamination---ConStat \\citep{constat2024}, DCR \\citep{dcr2025}, LogProber \\citep{logprober2024}, Min-K\\% \\citep{mink2024}---but all assume a fixed rater, so judge severity can masquerade as contamination. \\citet{preference_leakage2026} identify judge-side contamination (same-family leakage), complementary to G-MFRM's examinee-side focus.\n\n\\paragraph{Contamination Avoidance.}\nLiveBench \\citep{livebench2025}, TRUCE \\citep{truce2024}, and tinyBenchmarks \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks} prevent or reduce contamination by design; G-MFRM corrects for residual contamination in existing benchmarks.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT and MFRM for LLM Evaluation.}\n\\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} apply MFRM to LLM raters without contamination terms; \\citet{uto2020generalized}, \\citet{choi2026irt_judge}, and PSN-IRT \\citep{zhou2025psnirt} extend IRT/MFRM along other axes but none include contamination facets. The closest antecedent, DG-IRT \\citep{shu2013dgirt}, is compared in Section~\\ref{sec:model_spec}.",

[Director 指令] [Patch] 数据审计发现 CRITICAL 问题：Appendix synthetic calibration 表使用了 12-seed 中间数据

**需要修正**: appendix.tex app:synthetic_calibration 表格（约 lines 370-372 附近）

用以下 20-seed 最终数据替换：

| Metric | Sequential (20-seed) | Joint G-MFRM (20-seed) |
|--------|---------------------|----------------------|
| θ RMSE | 0.166 ± 0.050 | 0.083 ± 0.024 |
| θ τ | 0.882 ± 0.112 | 0.954 ± 0.048 |
| c RMSE | 1.399 ± 0.358 | 0.552 ± 0.328 |
| c Pearson | 0.850 ± 0.141 | 0.866 ± 0.151 |

**具体变化**：
- c_n RMSE sequential: 1.349 → 1.399
- c_n RMSE joint: 0.503 → 0.552
- c_n improvement: -62.7% → -60.5%
- θ_τ std sequential: 0.117 → 0.112
- θ_τ std joint: 0.046 → 0.048
- θ_RMSE std sequential: 0.049 → 0.050

θ RMSE mean 和 θ τ mean 不变（0.166→0.083, 0.882→0.954 正确）。

**同时检查**：正文（experiments.tex）和 abstract 中是否引用了 1.349、0.503、-62.7%、-63% 这些旧数值，如果有也需要更新。Abstract 中 "51.2%" 是 N=50 数据不受影响。

358	\section{Synthetic Calibration at Matched Scale}
359	\label{app:synthetic_calibration}
360	
361	We generate synthetic data at matched scale ($N = 8$ models, $I = 176$ items, $J = 9$ judges) with known ground-truth parameters across 20 random seeds. Joint G-MFRM consistently outperforms sequential estimation: $\theta$-RMSE is halved ($0.083$ vs.\ $0.166$), and ranking agreement improves by 7.2 percentage points (mean $\tau = 0.954$ vs.\ $0.882$). Across 20 seeds, joint estimation improves or matches sequential in all cases (10 wins, 0 losses, 10 ties; Wilcoxon signed-rank $p < 0.002$). The synthetic effect size ($\Delta\tau = +0.072$) closely matches the observed real-data effect ($\Delta\tau = +0.071$). All 40 MCMC fits converge ($\hat{R} = 1.0$, ESS $\geq 1{,}678$).
362	
363	\begin{table}[h]
364	\centering
365	\small
366	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
367	\toprule
368	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Sequential} & \textbf{Joint G-MFRM} & \textbf{Improv.} \\
369	\midrule
370	$\theta$ RMSE & $0.166 \pm 0.049$ & $0.083 \pm 0.024$ & $-50.1\%$ \\
371	$\theta$ $\tau$ & $0.882 \pm 0.117$ & $0.954 \pm 0.046$ & $+7.2$pp \\
372	$c_n$ RMSE & $1.349$ & $0.503$ & $-62.7\%$ \\
373	\bottomrule
374	\end{tabular}
375	\caption{Synthetic calibration at matched scale ($N = 8$, $I = 176$, $J = 9$, 20 seeds). Joint G-MFRM halves $\theta$-RMSE and reduces $c_n$-RMSE by 63\% relative to sequential estimation.}
376	\label{tab:synthetic_calibration}
377	\end{table}
378	
379	
380	\section{CI Coverage Analysis}
381	\label{app:ci_coverage}
382

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MPYNVbjGNCiSnrya9A7JLy`</summary>

```text
361	We generate synthetic data at matched scale ($N = 8$ models, $I = 176$ items, $J = 9$ judges) with known ground-truth parameters across 20 random seeds. Joint G-MFRM consistently outperforms sequential estimation: $\theta$-RMSE is halved ($0.083$ vs.\ $0.166$), and ranking agreement improves by 7.2 percentage points (mean $\tau = 0.954$ vs.\ $0.882$). Across 20 seeds, joint estimation improves or matches sequential in all cases (10 wins, 0 losses, 10 ties; Wilcoxon signed-rank $p < 0.002$). The synthetic effect size ($\Delta\tau = +0.072$) closely matches the observed real-data effect ($\Delta\tau = +0.071$). All 40 MCMC fits converge ($\hat{R} = 1.0$, ESS $\geq 1{,}678$).
362	
```

**exp-018 完成，结果极强**。N=20 下 Δτ Wilcoxon p=1.81e-4 (18/1/1)，完美验证 power analysis 的 N≈17 for 80% power 预测。

**需要修改 3 处**：

### 1. Appendix app:synthetic_calibration — 扩展为 N=8 + N=20 对比

在现有 N=8 表格后面新增一个 N=20 表格或合并为一个表格：

N=20 数据 (exp-018, 20 seeds):
| Metric | Sequential | Joint G-MFRM | Improv. |
|--------|-----------|-------------|---------|
| θ RMSE | 0.171 ± 0.049 | 0.094 ± 0.019 | -45.0% |
| θ τ | 0.891 ± 0.038 | 0.943 ± 0.036 | +5.2pp |
| c_n RMSE | 1.375 ± 0.278 | 0.626 ± 0.263 | -54.5% |
| c_n Pearson | 0.817 ± 0.096 | 0.842 ± 0.106 | +2.5% |

关键统计检验：Δτ Wilcoxon p = 1.81e-4 (18 wins / 1 tie / 1 loss)

文字要点：
- N=20 效应量 Δτ=+0.052 与 N=8 的 +0.072 跨 N 稳定
- p-value 从 N=8 的 0.002 下降到 N=20 的 1.81e-4
- 直接验证论文 power analysis 预测

### 2. experiments.tex line 48 — 在 power analysis 句后加 N=20 验证

当前文本提到 "N≈17 is needed for 80% power"。在这句后面加：
"This prediction is empirically validated: at N=20, synthetic Δτ reaches significance (Wilcoxon p < 0.001, 18 wins / 1 loss; Appendix~\ref{app:synthetic_calibration})."

### 3. 检查 abstract — 不需要改
Abstract 已经没有引用 N≈17 的具体数字。如果能自然地加一句提到 N=20 验证就加，否则不改。

```text
358	\section{Synthetic Calibration at Matched Scale}
359	\label{app:synthetic_calibration}
360	
361	We generate synthetic data at matched scale ($N = 8$ models, $I = 176$ items, $J = 9$ judges) with known ground-truth parameters across 20 random seeds. Joint G-MFRM consistently outperforms sequential estimation: $\theta$-RMSE is halved ($0.083$ vs.\ $0.166$), and ranking agreement improves by 7.2 percentage points (mean $\tau = 0.954$ vs.\ $0.882$). Across 20 seeds, joint estimation improves or matches sequential in all cases (10 wins, 0 losses, 10 ties; Wilcoxon signed-rank $p < 0.002$). The synthetic effect size ($\Delta\tau = +0.072$) closely matches the observed real-data effect ($\Delta\tau = +0.071$). All 40 MCMC fits converge ($\hat{R} = 1.0$, ESS $\geq 1{,}678$).
362	
363	\begin{table}[h]
364	\centering
365	\small
366	\begin{tabular}{lccc}
367	\toprule
368	\textbf{Metric} & \textbf{Sequential} & \textbf{Joint G-MFRM} & \textbf{Improv.} \\
369	\midrule
370	$\theta$ RMSE & $0.166 \pm 0.050$ & $0.083 \pm 0.024$ & $-50.1\%$ \\
371	$\theta$ $\tau$ & $0.882 \pm 0.112$ & $0.954 \pm 0.048$ & $+7.2$pp \\
372	$c_n$ RMSE & $1.399 \pm 0.358$ & $0.552 \pm 0.328$ & $-60.5\%$ \\
373	$c_n$ Pearson & $0.850 \pm 0.141$ & $0.866 \pm 0.151$ & $+1.9$\% \\
374	\bottomrule
375	\end{tabular}
376	\caption{Synthetic calibration at matched scale ($N = 8$, $I = 176$, $J = 9$, 20 seeds). Joint G-MFRM halves $\theta$-RMSE and reduces $c_n$-RMSE by 61\% relative to sequential estimation.}
377	\label{tab:synthetic_calibration}
378	\end{table}
379	
380	
381	\section{CI Coverage Analysis}
382	\label{app:ci_coverage}
383	
384	We assess empirical coverage of 95\% posterior credible intervals across synthetic replications. At $N=8$ (49 seeds), ability parameter $\theta$ achieves 90.1\% coverage, approaching the nominal level, while contamination susceptibility $c_n$ achieves only 67.9\%. At $N=50$ (50 seeds; exp-009), $c_n$ coverage improves to 71\%. The coverage gap between $\theta$ and $c_n$ reflects the multiplicative coupling $c_n \cdot e_i$: contamination parameters enter the likelihood through an interaction term, making posterior inference more sensitive to exposure estimation noise and the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint at $c_n = 0$. These results support treating $c_n$ point estimates and rank orderings as reliable diagnostics, while noting that $c_n$ credible intervals are systematically too narrow.
385	
```

_source line 3905 · 2026-05-23T11:42:02.520Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_019Rxgr9z5DpQ9RQYz6EWjqu`</summary>

```text
46	LOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM ($\Delta\mathrm{ELPD}_{\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, SE $= 9.4$, $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable LOO estimates. The contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.
47	
48	Ranking improvement ($\Delta\tau = +0.071$) is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\% CI crosses zero). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \approx 17$ is needed for 80\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\ref{app:power}). Synthetic replication at matched scale ($N = 8$, $I = 176$, $J = 9$) confirms a significant effect across 20 independent synthetic replications (Wilcoxon $p < 0.002$; Appendix~\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}), demonstrating that the method consistently improves rankings though any single $N = 8$ dataset has limited power to detect this improvement.
49	
50	The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\hat{R} \leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to posterior overconfidence (synthetic CI coverage 71\%; see Limitations); we report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age, directionally consistent with known training data exposure. Estimated $\hat{c}_n$ exceeds 1.0 for the four oldest models: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($\hat{c}_n = 5.60$), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$). The most recent models show low estimated contamination: GPT-4o ($0.88$) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$). The global shrinkage scale $\hat{\sigma}_c = 2.40$ confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\bar{c} \approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\hat{\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}). A per-model diagnostic (Table~\ref{tab:bias_absorption}) reveals that MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (GPT-3.5-Turbo), while deflating estimates for the least contaminated models by up to 0.40 logits (GPT-4o). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \geq 2$, with $\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\ref{app:judge_count}).
```

Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).",

_source line 3907 · 2026-05-23T11:42:03.337Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01GCSrtRn1tWyL9WbU7g87N7`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-as-a-judge evaluation corrects for judge bias and benchmark contamination independently, but sequential correction produces biased model ability estimates. We formalize this failure as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem: when judge severity and contamination effects are both present, any estimator that models one while omitting the other yields asymptotically biased ability estimates---even when the two facets are uncorrelated. We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly calibrates model ability, item difficulty, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility through staged estimation with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators. Formal identifiability analysis proves that fixing exposure externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs. Error propagation analysis establishes a critical noise threshold $\sigma_{\mathrm{crit}} = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$ below which ability estimates degrade by less than 6\%. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$). On Arena-Hard with 8 target models and 9 judges, G-MFRM identifies per-model contamination signals (estimated $c_n > 1.0$ for top-4 models, directionally consistent with known training data exposure; posterior CI coverage is 71\% in synthetic validation) and interpretable judge severity profiles, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$ vs.\ $0.786$; bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ CI includes zero at this sample size). LOO cross-validation confirms improved predictive fit for G-MFRM over standard MFRM ($\Delta\mathrm{ELPD} = -55.7$, $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$).
3	\end{abstract}
```

        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " ",
          " LOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD}_{\\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, SE $= 9.4$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \\citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable LOO estimates. The contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.",
          " ",
          "-Ranking improvement ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$) is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI crosses zero). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \\approx 17$ is needed for 80\\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\\ref{app:power}). Synthetic replication at matched scale ($N = 8$, $I = 176$, $J = 9$) confirms a significant effect across 20 independent synthetic replications (Wilcoxon $p < 0.002$; Appendix~\\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}), demonstrating that the method consistently improves rankings though any single $N = 8$ dataset has limited power to detect this improvement.",
          "+Ranking improvement ($\\Delta\\tau = +0.071$) is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\\% CI crosses zero). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \\approx 17$ is needed for 80\\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\\ref{app:power}). This prediction is empirically validated: at $N = 20$, synthetic $\\Delta\\tau$ reaches significance (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$, 18 wins / 1 loss; Appendix~\\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}). Pooling 20 replications at $N = 8$ also yields significance ($p < 0.002$), confirming the method consistently improves rankings though any single $N = 8$ dataset has limited power.",
          " ",
          " The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\\hat{R} \\leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \\citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \\citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to posterior overconfidence (synthetic CI coverage 71\\%; see Limitations); we report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age, directionally consistent with known training data exposure. Estimated $\\hat{c}_n$ exceeds 1.0 for the four oldest models: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($\\hat{c}_n = 5.60$), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$). The most recent models show low estimated contamination: GPT-4o ($0.88$) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$). The global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). A per-model diagnostic (Table~\\ref{tab:bias_absorption}) reveals that MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (GPT-3.5-Turbo), while deflating estimates for the least contaminated models by up to 0.40 logits (GPT-4o). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).",
          " "
        ]
      }
    ],

[Director 指令] Round 4 审稿写作修复（不依赖 N 扩展，立即可做）

Round 4 fresh review 3.0。以下写作修复不受 N 扩展影响，请立即执行：

## 1. Abstract 超 200 词 [MUST-FIX, 格式违规]
当前 ~209-213 词，EMNLP 2026 硬限 ≤200 词。压缩至 ≤195 词。
策略：删减 LOO 细节或合并 synthetic/real 结果描述。不要删核心 claim。

## 2. §4.2 LOO vs Ranking 物理分隔 [W7, 复发2轮]
当前两条证据线（predictive fit vs ranking accuracy）在同段落。审稿人说分离不足。
用 \paragraph{Predictive fit.} 和 \paragraph{Ranking accuracy.} 物理分隔这两个论点。

## 3. OVB 线性近似讨论 [W10]
§2 Observation 1 附近增加 1-2 句：明确说线性近似（r=0.86）仅用于 motivation/方向性论证，G-MFRM Eq.4 使用完整非线性 ordinal logistic model，不依赖此近似。

## 4. "First joint" claim 限定 [W11]
在 Introduction 或 Method 中出现 "first joint contamination-severity diagnostic" 的地方，加限定 "within a measurement-theoretic framework"。Li et al. (2024, Preference Leakage) 已识别 judge-specific contamination patterns，"first" 需要精确限定范围。

## 5. W5 循环性反驳 [新增段落]
在 §4.2 或 §3.2 加一段（3-4句）说明 contamination 验证不是循环的：
- e_i 是 item-level exposure（外部锚定），c_n 是 model-level susceptibility
- 模型检验的是 cross-level interaction：哪些模型在高曝光 items 上有不成比例的表现偏差
- 关键证据：exp-013 100%/0% 权重配置（纯 reference anchoring，完全不用 cross-model agreement）下 exposure ρ = 0.998，去掉潜在循环分量后 e_i 几乎不变

## 6. CI coverage 讨论增强 [W2]
在 Limitations 中扩展 CI coverage 讨论：
- 明确说 68-71% coverage 不随 N 改善，是 HalfNormal boundary at c_n=0 + multiplicative c_n·e_i 的结构性问题
- 将 c_n 定位为 directional diagnostic（relative ordering 可信），CI 作为 lower bound
- 提及 SBC 校正和 asymmetric posterior 作为未来方向

## 约束
- 正文 ≤ 8 页
- 编译后检查无断裂引用
- 不要改任何实验数字（这些可能因 N 扩展而变）

```text
1	We identify six limitations. First, although Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only a connected design, the current evaluation achieves 44.7\% fill rate (5{,}661 of 12{,}672 potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items. Relatedly, sparse observations per item produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Second, Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), and exposure estimate quality depends on the anchor remaining uncontaminated relative to the benchmark; as benchmarks age, anchor validity should be periodically re-assessed. Third, the current evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with eight target models; post-hoc power analysis indicates that detecting $\Delta\tau = 0.071$ at $\alpha = 0.05$ requires $N \approx 17$ models for 80\% power, while $N = 8$ yields approximately 50\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Fourth, empirical CI coverage differs by parameter type: ability estimates $\theta$ approach nominal coverage at moderate sample sizes (${\sim}$90\% at $N = 8$), while contamination susceptibility $c_n$ exhibits moderate posterior overconfidence (71\% at $N = 50$, 68\% at $N = 8$; \citealp{talts2018sbc}). The $c_n$ under-coverage reflects the multiplicative coupling $c_n \cdot e_i$ and the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint at $c_n = 0$, which systematically narrows posterior credible intervals. Point estimates and rank orderings ($\tau = 0.857$) are unaffected; $c_n$ estimates are best interpreted as directional diagnostics rather than calibrated intervals. Fifth, while self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, a more comprehensive analysis removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted. Sixth, the bilinear contamination term $c_n \cdot e_i$ assumes rank-1 structure---a single latent dimension of contamination susceptibility per model. In practice, contamination may be domain-specific (e.g., coding vs.\ reasoning items), requiring a higher-rank decomposition $\sum_r c_{nr} \cdot e_{ir}$ that the current model does not capture.
2	
3	Code and data will be released upon acceptance at [anonymized URL].
4

### Tool result

_source line 3951 · 2026-05-23T13:44:26.614Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_015SFq8APNo2v9s4KUovKYte`</summary>

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	LLM-as-a-judge evaluation corrects for judge bias and benchmark contamination independently, but sequential correction produces biased model ability estimates. We formalize this failure as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem: when judge severity and contamination effects are both present, any estimator that models one while omitting the other yields asymptotically biased ability estimates---even when the two facets are uncorrelated. We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly calibrates model ability, item difficulty, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility through staged estimation with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators. Formal identifiability analysis proves that fixing exposure externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs. Error propagation analysis establishes a critical noise threshold $\sigma_{\mathrm{crit}} = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$ below which ability estimates degrade by less than 6\%. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$). On Arena-Hard with 8 target models and 9 judges, G-MFRM identifies per-model contamination signals (estimated $c_n > 1.0$ for top-4 models, directionally consistent with known training data exposure; posterior CI coverage is 71\% in synthetic validation) and interpretable judge severity profiles, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$ vs.\ $0.786$; bootstrap $\Delta\tau$ CI includes zero at this sample size). LOO cross-validation confirms improved predictive fit for G-MFRM over standard MFRM ($\Delta\mathrm{ELPD} = -55.7$, $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$).
3	\end{abstract}
```

### Tool result

_source line 3953 · 2026-05-23T13:44:27.651Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01KZWzrwfn5H5TDZwvkiVpsh`</summary>

```text
44	We evaluate G-MFRM along two orthogonal axes: predictive fit and ranking accuracy.
45	
46	LOO cross-validation strongly favors G-MFRM ($\Delta\mathrm{ELPD}_{\mathrm{LOO}} = -55.7$, SE $= 9.4$, $|\Delta/\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$; \citealp{vehtari2017loo}), with stacking weight 1.0 for G-MFRM versus 0.0 for MFRM-only. All Pareto $\hat{k} < 0.7$, confirming reliable LOO estimates. The contamination gating term substantially improves held-out predictive fit.
47	
48	Ranking improvement ($\Delta\tau = +0.071$) is not statistically significant at $N = 8$ (bootstrap 95\% CI crosses zero). Post-hoc power analysis indicates $N \approx 17$ is needed for 80\% power to detect this effect size (Appendix~\ref{app:power}). This prediction is empirically validated: at $N = 20$, synthetic $\Delta\tau$ reaches significance (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$, 18 wins / 1 loss; Appendix~\ref{app:synthetic_calibration}). Pooling 20 replications at $N = 8$ also yields significance ($p < 0.002$), confirming the method consistently improves rankings though any single $N = 8$ dataset has limited power.
49	
50	The MCMC sampler achieves rank-normalized split-$\hat{R} \leq 1.03$ across all parameters with zero divergent transitions and minimum bulk ESS of 174 (217 for model-level parameters $\theta_n$, $c_n$, $d_j$). These values satisfy the traditional convergence thresholds ($\hat{R} < 1.05$, ESS $> 100$; \citealp{gelman2013bda}) but do not meet the stricter criteria of \citet{vehtari2021rhat} ($\hat{R} < 1.01$, ESS $> 400$), which may contribute to posterior overconfidence (synthetic CI coverage 71\%; see Limitations); we report point estimates and rank orderings as primary results. Model and judge facets show acceptable fit (all infit/outfit MNSQ within $[0.63, 1.07]$; item-level diagnostics in Appendix). Posterior predictive checks confirm adequate model fit (predicted response rate $0.593$ vs.\ observed $0.599$; Appendix~\ref{app:ppc}). Contamination susceptibility $\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age, directionally consistent with known training data exposure. Estimated $\hat{c}_n$ exceeds 1.0 for the four oldest models: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($\hat{c}_n = 5.60$), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$). The most recent models show low estimated contamination: GPT-4o ($0.88$) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$). The global shrinkage scale $\hat{\sigma}_c = 2.40$ confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\bar{c} \approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\hat{\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\ref{sec:confounding}). A per-model diagnostic (Table~\ref{tab:bias_absorption}) reveals that MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (GPT-3.5-Turbo), while deflating estimates for the least contaminated models by up to 0.40 logits (GPT-4o). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \geq 2$, with $\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\ref{app:judge_count}).
51

Contamination susceptibility $\\hat{c}_n$ exhibits a gradient by model age, directionally consistent with known training data exposure. Estimated $\\hat{c}_n$ exceeds 1.0 for the four oldest models: GPT-3.5-Turbo ($\\hat{c}_n = 5.60$), GPT-4-0613 ($4.20$), Qwen2.5-72B ($3.00$), and LLaMA-3.3-70B ($2.50$). The most recent models show low estimated contamination: GPT-4o ($0.88$) and GPT-4o-mini ($0.93$). The global shrinkage scale $\\hat{\\sigma}_c = 2.40$ confirms substantial inter-model heterogeneity ($\\bar{c} = 2.50$, aligning with the strong-contamination regime in synthetic validation). Notably, sequential estimation yields near-zero contamination estimates ($\\bar{c} \\approx 0$), demonstrating that without joint modeling, contamination effects are entirely absorbed into ability estimates $\\hat{\\theta}$---precisely the omitted variable bias predicted by the theoretical analysis (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}). A per-model diagnostic (Table~\\ref{tab:bias_absorption}) reveals that MFRM-only inflates ability estimates for the most contaminated models by up to 0.79 logits (GPT-3.5-Turbo), while deflating estimates for the least contaminated models by up to 0.40 logits (GPT-4o). Judge severity $d_j$ separates by provenance: proprietary judges tend to score more strictly (e.g., Claude Opus $+0.60$, GPT-5.5 $+0.53$) than open-source judges (e.g., Mistral $-0.74$, LLaMA $-0.42$); full $d_j$ values for all nine judges are reported in Table~\\ref{tab:judge_severity}. Results are robust to prior specification (Horseshoe vs.\\ HalfNormal: Pearson $r > 0.999$; Appendix) and to judge panel size: G-MFRM outperforms baselines at all judge counts $k \\geq 2$, with $\\tau$ increasing monotonically from $0.771$ ($k = 2$) to $0.857$ ($k = 9$); $k \\geq 4$ suffices to exceed the full-panel baseline (Appendix~\\ref{app:judge_count}).\n",

    }

### Tool result

_source line 3963 · 2026-05-23T13:44:41.526Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SDwfxS4onCukWgxWJNQ7mt`</summary>

```text
20	When a practitioner fits the standard MFRM (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}) to data generated by Eq.~\ref{eq:full}, the contamination term is absent from the estimated model. Its influence does not vanish but is absorbed into the estimated parameters $\tilde{\theta}_n$, $\tilde{b}_i$, and $\tilde{d}_j$, which distorts all downstream inferences.
21	
22	The consequence of this omission can be stated precisely.
23	
24	\begin{observation}[Omitted Variable Bias in MFRM]
25	\label{obs:ovb}
26	Let the full model be $\eta_{nijk} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i$ and the short model omit the contamination term: $\tilde{\eta}_{nijk} = \tilde{\theta}_n - \tilde{b}_i - \tilde{d}_j - \tau_k$. Under a linear approximation to the ordinal logit link \citep{wooldridge2010, angrist2009mostly},\footnote{The OVB formula is exact for linear models. We apply it to the MFRM linear predictor $\eta$, which is an approximation. Synthetic validation confirms the approximation quality: the Pearson correlation between the predicted bias direction and empirical bias reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), indicating that the linear result accurately characterizes bias direction and relative magnitude.}
27	\begin{equation}
28	\label{eq:ovb}
29	\mathrm{plim}(\tilde{\theta}_n - \theta_n) = \gamma_n \cdot c_n,
30	\end{equation}
31	where $\gamma_n$ depends on the projection of the exposure vector $(e_i)$ onto the design matrix of the short model (explicit form in Appendix~\ref{app:gamma}). The bias is non-zero whenever $\mathrm{Cov}(d_j,\, c_n \cdot e_i) \neq 0$. This captures the confounding component; in the nonlinear MFRM, a specification bias from omitting $c_n \cdot e_i$ persists even at zero covariance (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
32	\end{observation}
33	
34	The linear approximation in Observation~\ref{obs:ovb} holds when $c_n \cdot e_i$ is small relative to the logit scale; for large contamination effects, the gated model (Eq.~\ref{eq:gmfrm}) captures the full nonlinear interaction without relying on this approximation. The bias combines a specification component from omitting $c_n \cdot e_i$, which persists regardless of confounding, with a confounding component from non-orthogonality between $d_j$ and $c_n \cdot e_i$. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that bias magnitude is approximately constant across confounding levels $\rho \in \{0, 0.3, 0.6, 0.9\}$, indicating that the omission of $c_n \cdot e_i$---not its correlation with $d_j$---is the primary driver (Figure~\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho}).
35	
36	This confounding undermines both sequential estimation strategies. A bias-first approach, as in CyclicJudge \citep{cyclicjudge2026}, calibrates $d_j$ before estimating ability, but the resulting $\hat{\theta}$ is already inflated for contaminated models because the omitted $c_n \cdot e_i$ term has been absorbed into the ability estimates. A contamination-first approach, as in DCR \citep{dcr2025}, adjusts for contamination before addressing judge effects, but judge severity is then misattributed as contamination signal. Synthetic validation confirms this failure: with $N = 50$ models, $I = 100$ items, $J = 8$ judges, and 20 random seeds, the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$). As illustrated in Figure~\ref{fig:ovb_bias}, the bias is not random but directional: contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings.
37	
38	\begin{figure}[t]
39	\centering
40	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig2_ovb_bias}
41	\caption{Sequential estimation bias ($\hat{\theta}_{\mathrm{seq}} - \theta_{\mathrm{true}}$) versus true contamination susceptibility $c_n$. Pearson $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$) confirms systematic overestimation of contaminated models. Synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds).}
42	\label{fig:ovb_bias}
43	\end{figure}
```

14	    \item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\ref{prop:no_new}--\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\ref{sec:method}, Table~\ref{tab:error_prop}).
15	    \item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$), with Kendall rank correlation $\tau = 0.922$ versus $\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
16	    \item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$ vs.\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).
17	\end{enumerate}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01HkehZS3Fe1uFiuXxk6mmZe`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	We introduced G-MFRM, which jointly estimates model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility by formalizing their confounding as omitted variable bias. On Arena-Hard, G-MFRM produces the first joint contamination--severity diagnostic, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\tau = 0.857$ vs.\ $0.786$; not significant at $N = 8$); well-powered validation requires $N \geq 20$ (Limitations). Treating judge bias and contamination as separable leads to systematic estimation errors; future LLM evaluation frameworks should model these facets jointly.
5	
```

</details>

1	\section{The G-MFRM Framework}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	Section~\ref{sec:confounding} established that omitting contamination from the measurement model produces directional bias in ability estimates (Observation~\ref{obs:ovb}). We now introduce the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly estimates ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility within a single measurement framework. The key design choices, staged estimation with externally fixed exposure and a hierarchical HalfNormal prior for the contamination term, are motivated by the identifiability analysis in \S\ref{sec:identifiability} and the error propagation bounds in \S\ref{sec:error_prop}.
5	
6	\begin{figure}[t]
7	\centering
8	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig1_framework}
9	\caption{Overview of the G-MFRM three-stage calibration procedure. Stage~1 fits a standard MFRM to obtain initial estimates $(\hat{\theta}, \hat{b}, \hat{d})$. Stage~2 estimates per-item exposure $e_i$ from external evidence (reference model anchoring and cross-model agreement). Stage~3 performs joint MCMC inference with $e_i$ fixed, recovering contamination-corrected ability $\tilde{\theta}_n$, contamination susceptibility $c_n$, and judge severity $d_j$.}
10	\label{fig:framework}
11	\end{figure}
12	
13	\subsection{Model Specification}
14	\label{sec:model_spec}
15	
16	G-MFRM extends the standard MFRM \citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm} by adding a multiplicative contamination gating term to the linear predictor (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}):
17	%
18	\begin{equation}
19	\label{eq:gmfrm}
20	\log \frac{P(Y_{nij} \geq k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k)} = \theta_n - b_i - d_j - \tau_k + c_n \cdot e_i,
21	\end{equation}
22	%
23	where all terms carry the definitions from Section~\ref{sec:confounding}. The gating structure $c_n \cdot e_i$ separates the model-specific and item-specific components of contamination: $c_n \geq 0$ captures how much model $n$ benefits from memorized benchmark content, while $e_i \in [0,1]$ quantifies how much of item $i$ has leaked into training corpora. When $c_n = 0$ (a clean model) or $e_i = 0$ (a novel item), the gating term vanishes and Eq.~\ref{eq:gmfrm} reduces to the standard MFRM. This graceful degradation ensures that G-MFRM imposes no distortion on uncontaminated model-item pairs.
24	
25	We enforce non-negativity and sparsity of $c_n$ through a hierarchical HalfNormal prior. A global shrinkage scale $\sigma_c \sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ controls the overall contamination level, and per-model raw effects $c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0)$ are scaled to obtain $c_n = c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \cdot \sigma_c$. The HalfNormal support restricts $c_n \geq 0$, and the hierarchical structure provides adaptive shrinkage: when the data support low overall contamination, $\sigma_c$ contracts toward zero, pulling all $c_n$ values down. Models with genuine contamination retain large $c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n$ relative to the group. The remaining priors are $\theta_n \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $b_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 1)$, $d_j \sim \mathcal{N}(0, 0.5)$, and standard uniform on thresholds $\tau_k$.
26	
27	The closest antecedent is the Doubly-Gated IRT model of \citet{shu2013dgirt}, which introduced binary gating for item preknowledge in educational testing. Beyond extending DG-IRT to continuous exposure and multi-rater designs, G-MFRM's primary methodological contributions are analytical: the identifiability analysis (Propositions~\ref{prop:no_new}--\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\ref{thm:sufficient}) provides the first formal conditions under which gated IRT parameters are separable, and the Murphy--Topel error propagation framework (\S\ref{sec:error_prop}) quantifies how staged estimation uncertainty propagates to downstream parameter estimates---neither of which exists for DG-IRT or any prior gated measurement model.
28	
29	\subsection{Staged Estimation}
30	\label{sec:staged}
31	
32	Joint estimation of all parameters in Eq.~\ref{eq:gmfrm}, including $e_i$, would require identifying the bilinear product $c_n \cdot e_i$ from response data alone. This creates a fundamental non-identifiability (Proposition~\ref{prop:exposure_variation}). We resolve this through a three-stage procedure that fixes exposure externally before estimating the contamination parameters.
33	
34	\paragraph{Stage 1: Standard MFRM.} We fit the standard MFRM (Eq.~\ref{eq:mfrm}) via MCMC (NUTS) to obtain initial estimates $(\hat{\theta}, \hat{b}, \hat{d})$. These serve as warm-start values for the sampler in Stage~3 and improve convergence.
35	
36	\paragraph{Stage 2: Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated from external evidence independent of the response data used in Stages~1 and~3. The primary signal is \emph{reference model anchoring}: using a fixed reference model (e.g., gpt-4-0314) as anchor, we compute win-rate residuals for each target model against difficulty-adjusted expectations; items where target models systematically outperform the anchor beyond what difficulty predicts are flagged as potentially exposed. The secondary signal is \emph{cross-model agreement}: items receiving uniformly high scores across architecturally diverse models suggest exposure rather than genuine easiness, quantified via $z$-score standardization of the inter-model agreement distribution. These signals are combined as a weighted composite (75\% anchoring, 25\% agreement), clipped to $[0, 1]$, and treated as fixed in Stage~3. Where model log-probabilities are accessible, LogProber can provide an additional gray-box memorization signal, though it is not used in our current experiments.
37	
38	\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via NUTS \citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \S\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as
39	%
40	\begin{align}
41	\label{eq:contam_prior}
42	\sigma_c &\sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \quad c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \sim \text{HalfNormal}(1.0), \nonumber \\
43	c_n &= c^{\mathrm{raw}}_n \cdot \sigma_c,
44	\end{align}
45	%
46	where $\sigma_c$ is a shared global scale that governs the overall contamination magnitude. This non-centered parameterization improves sampler geometry by decoupling the global scale from the per-model effects.
47	
48	Convergence is assessed via rank-normalized split-$\hat{R}$ \citep{vehtari2021rhat} and bulk effective sample size (ESS), with thresholds $\hat{R} < 1.05$ and ESS$_{\mathrm{bulk}} > 100$ following standard practice \citep{gelman2013bda}.\footnote{Synthetic validation with $N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$ yields satisfactory convergence across all parameters (exp-012). Full diagnostic tables are reported in Section~\ref{sec:experiments}.}
49	
50	
51	\subsection{Identifiability}
52	\label{sec:identifiability}
53	
54	Adding a multiplicative term to a measurement model raises the question of whether the new parameters can be separated from the existing ones. We establish that fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for identification. All proofs assume the standard location constraint $\sum_n \theta_n = 0$.
55	
56	\begin{proposition}[No New Indeterminacy]
57	\label{prop:no_new}
58	When $e_i$ is fixed externally, the gating term $c_n \cdot e_i$ introduces no additional location or scale indeterminacy beyond that already present in the standard MFRM.
59	\end{proposition}
60	
61	\begin{proof}
62	Suppose $c_n' = c_n + \delta$ preserves all response probabilities. Then $\delta \cdot e_i = 0$ for all items. Since $e_i \neq 0$ for at least one item by assumption, $\delta = 0$. The key observation is that fixing $e_i$ externally eliminates the bilinear ambiguity $c_n \cdot e_i = (\lambda c_n)(\lambda^{-1} e_i)$ that would otherwise render the decomposition non-unique.
63	\end{proof}
64	
65	\begin{proposition}[Exposure Variation Necessary]
66	\label{prop:exposure_variation}
67	If $e_i = e$ for all $i$ (constant exposure), then $c_n$ and $\theta_n$ are not separately identifiable.
68	\end{proposition}
69	
70	\begin{proof}
71	When exposure is constant, the linear predictor becomes $(\theta_n + c_n \cdot e) - b_i - d_j - \tau_k$, which is equivalent to a standard MFRM with reparameterized ability $\theta^*_n = \theta_n + c_n \cdot e$. No configuration of response data can distinguish $(\theta_n, c_n)$ from $(\theta_n + \epsilon, c_n - \epsilon/e)$ for any $\epsilon$.
72	\end{proof}
73	
74	\begin{corollary}
75	$\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ is a necessary condition for identifiability of $c_n$.
76	\end{corollary}
77	
78	\begin{theorem}[Sufficient Conditions for Local Identifiability]
79	\label{thm:sufficient}
80	The G-MFRM is locally identifiable, up to the standard location constraint, if the following conditions hold: (i) a connected observation design (each item is observed by at least one model--judge pair), (ii) $J \geq 2$ judges, (iii) for each model $n$, the set of observed items spans at least two distinct exposure values ($\mathrm{Var}(e_i) > 0$ over observed items), and (iv) standard normalization $\sum_n \theta_n = 0$.
81	\end{theorem}
82	
83	The proof, given in Appendix~\ref{app:proof}, follows \citet{rothenberg1971}: non-singularity of the Fisher information matrix is established via the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality on the $(\theta_n, c_n)$ submatrix determinant and a Schur complement argument for the remaining blocks. Conditions~(i) and~(ii) are standard multi-rater design requirements, satisfied by the connected design in our evaluation (44.7\% fill rate; \S\ref{sec:setup}); condition~(iii) is the substantive constraint that each model's observed items include varying exposure levels.
84	
85	\subsection{Error Propagation}
86	\label{sec:error_prop}
87	
88	The staged procedure treats the Stage~2 exposure estimates $\hat{e}_i$ as fixed quantities in Stage~3. This is a generated regressors problem \citep{pagan1984}: measurement error in $\hat{e}_i$ propagates into the posterior of $(\theta_n, c_n)$. The multiplicative structure $c_n \cdot e_i$ makes this an errors-in-variables (EIV) problem \citep{fuller1987}, with a predictable attenuation pattern.
89	
90	Let $\hat{e}_i = e_i + \epsilon_i$ with $\epsilon_i \sim \mathcal{N}(0, \sigma^2)$ independent of all model parameters. The attenuation factor for the contamination coefficient is
91	%
92	\begin{equation}
93	\label{eq:attenuation}
94	\lambda(\sigma) = \frac{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}{\mathrm{Var}(e_i) + \sigma^2},
95	\end{equation}
96	%
97	so attenuation reaches 50\% when $\sigma = \sqrt{\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$. In the synthetic DGP ($\mathrm{Var}(e_i) \approx 0.04$), this corresponds to $\sigma \approx 0.2$.\footnote{The attenuation formula assumes that $\epsilon_i$ is independent of all model parameters, identically distributed, and Gaussian. In practice, exposure estimation errors may be correlated across items sharing the same source or heteroscedastic across difficulty levels. Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} provides empirical validation that $\hat{\theta}$ estimates remain robust under such departures (Section~\ref{sec:experiments}).}
98	
99	Table~\ref{tab:error_prop} shows the empirical degradation across noise levels (see also Figure~\ref{fig:error_prop} in the Appendix). Below the critical threshold ($\sigma \leq 0.2$), degradation is mild: $\theta$-RMSE increases by 5.9\% (from $0.128 \pm 0.018$ to $0.135 \pm 0.018$) and $c_n$-RMSE by 7.9\% (from $0.533 \pm 0.187$ to $0.575 \pm 0.198$). At $\sigma = 0.1$, the increases are only 1.4\% and 2.3\% respectively. Beyond the threshold ($\sigma = 0.5$), degradation becomes severe: $\theta$-RMSE rises 36.9\% and $c_n$-RMSE rises 57.9\%. Contamination estimates $c_n$ are more sensitive than ability estimates $\theta_n$ because $c_n$ enters the likelihood only through its product with $e_i$ (multiplicative coupling), while $\theta_n$ contributes additively and is anchored by all observations regardless of exposure.
100	
101	These bounds make the staged approximation falsifiable: if the noise level of the Stage~2 exposure estimates can be bounded below $\sigma_{\mathrm{crit}}$, the resulting ability estimates carry quantifiable error guarantees. For honest credible intervals that account for generated-regressor uncertainty, we apply the Murphy-Topel variance correction \citep{murphy1985} to inflate the Stage~3 posterior variance by the estimated Stage~2 sampling variability.
102	
103	\subsection{Fair Scores and Diagnostics}
104	\label{sec:fair_scores}
105	
106	The Stage~3 posterior mean of $\theta_n$ provides a bias-and-contamination-corrected ``fair score'' for each model. The remaining parameters serve complementary diagnostic roles: per-model $c_n$ flags candidates for training data audit, per-judge $d_j$ informs judge calibration, and per-item $e_i$ identifies items for retirement or replacement.
```

{
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
  "content": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-as-a-judge evaluation corrects for judge bias and benchmark contamination independently, but sequential correction produces biased model ability estimates. We formalize this failure as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem: when judge severity and contamination effects are both present, any estimator that models one while omitting the other yields asymptotically biased ability estimates---even when the two facets are uncorrelated. We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly calibrates model ability, item difficulty, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility through staged estimation with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators. Formal identifiability analysis proves that fixing exposure externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs. Error propagation analysis establishes a critical noise threshold below which ability estimates degrade by less than 6\\%. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$). On Arena-Hard with 8 target models and 9 judges, G-MFRM identifies per-model contamination signals directionally consistent with known training data exposure and interpretable judge severity profiles, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at this sample size). LOO cross-validation confirms improved predictive fit ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD} = -55.7$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$).\n\\end{abstract}\n"
}

We formalize this failure as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem: when judge severity and contamination effects are both present, any estimator that models one while omitting the other yields asymptotically biased ability estimates---even when the two facets are uncorrelated. We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly calibrates model ability, item difficulty, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility through staged estimation with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators. Formal identifiability analysis proves that fixing exposure externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs. Error propagation analysis establishes a critical noise threshold below which ability estimates degrade by less than 6\\%. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$). On Arena-Hard with 8 target models and 9 judges, G-MFRM identifies per-model contamination signals directionally consistent with known training data exposure and interpretable judge severity profiles, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at this sample size). LOO cross-validation confirms improved predictive fit ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD} = -55.7$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$).\n\\end{abstract}\n",

We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly calibrates model ability, item difficulty, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility through staged estimation with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators. Formal identifiability analysis proves that fixing exposure externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs. Error propagation analysis establishes a critical noise threshold $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}} = \\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$ below which ability estimates degrade by less than 6\\%. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$). On Arena-Hard with 8 target models and 9 judges, G-MFRM identifies per-model contamination signals (estimated $c_n > 1.0$ for top-4 models, directionally consistent with known training data exposure; posterior CI coverage is 71\\% in synthetic validation) and interpretable judge severity profiles, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at this sample size).     "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nLLM-as-a-judge evaluation corrects for judge bias and benchmark contamination independently, but sequential correction produces biased model ability estimates. We formalize this failure as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem: when judge severity and contamination effects are both present, any estimator that models one while omitting the other yields asymptotically biased ability estimates---even when the two facets are uncorrelated. We propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which jointly calibrates model ability, item difficulty, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility through staged estimation with externally anchored per-item exposure indicators. Formal identifiability analysis proves that fixing exposure externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs. Error propagation analysis establishes a critical noise threshold $\\sigma_{\\mathrm{crit}} = \\sqrt{\\mathrm{Var}(e_i)}$ below which ability estimates degrade by less than 6\\%. In synthetic experiments ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$), G-MFRM reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$). On Arena-Hard with 8 target models and 9 judges, G-MFRM identifies per-model contamination signals (estimated $c_n > 1.0$ for top-4 models, directionally consistent with known training data exposure; posterior CI coverage is 71\\% in synthetic validation) and interpretable judge severity profiles, with nominally higher rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$; bootstrap $\\Delta\\tau$ CI includes zero at this sample size). LOO cross-validation confirms improved predictive fit for G-MFRM over standard MFRM ($\\Delta\\mathrm{ELPD} = -55.7$, $|\\Delta/\\mathrm{SE}| = 5.9$).\n\\end{abstract}",
    "userModified": false
  },

    "newString": "The linear approximation in Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb} ($r = 0.86$) serves solely as directional motivation for the bias structure; G-MFRM's Stage~3 inference (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm}) uses the full nonlinear ordinal logistic likelihood and does not depend on this approximation.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Confounding in LLM Evaluation}\n\\label{sec:confounding}\n\nThe Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) extends classical Rasch measurement to settings with multiple rater facets \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, linacre1994mfrm}. Originating in educational measurement, the model treats rater variation as a nuisance facet to be estimated and removed rather than a source of noise to be averaged over. In the LLM evaluation context, where $J$ judges score $N$ models across $I$ items, the MFRM specifies\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:mfrm}\n\\log \\frac{P(Y_{nij} \\geq k \\mid \\theta_n, b_i, d_j, \\tau_k)}{P(Y_{nij} < k \\mid \\theta_n, b_i, d_j, \\tau_k)} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k,\n\\end{equation}\n%\nwhere $Y_{nij}$ is the ordinal rating by judge $j$ for model $n$ on item $i$, $\\theta_n$ is the ability of model $n$ ($n = 1, \\ldots, N$), $b_i$ is the difficulty of item $i$ ($i = 1, \\ldots, I$), $d_j$ is the severity of judge $j$ ($j = 1, \\ldots, J$), and $\\tau_k$ is the threshold for category $k$. All facets reside on a common logit scale, so model ability is directly comparable after adjustment for item difficulty and judge severity. \\citet{jiao2025mfrm_llm} applied this framework to compare ten LLM raters with human experts, showing that MFRM separates genuine ability from systematic judge effects in multi-judge panels.\n\nHowever, standard MFRM assumes that all systematic sources of score variation are captured by the modeled facets. This assumption breaks down when some models have been exposed to benchmark items during training \\citep{dcr2025}. Such contamination inflates scores on exposed items through memorization rather than genuine ability. We represent this effect as an interaction $c_n \\cdot e_i$, where $c_n \\geq 0$ is the contamination susceptibility of model $n$ and $e_i \\in [0, 1]$ is the exposure level of item $i$. The complete data-generating process becomes\n%\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:full}\n\\eta_{nijk} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i.\n\\end{equation}\n%\nWhen a practitioner fits the standard MFRM (Eq.~\\ref{eq:mfrm}) to data generated by Eq.~\\ref{eq:full}, the contamination term is absent from the estimated model. Its influence does not vanish but is absorbed into the estimated parameters $\\tilde{\\theta}_n$, $\\tilde{b}_i$, and $\\tilde{d}_j$, which distorts all downstream inferences.\n\nThe consequence of this omission can be stated precisely.\n\n\\begin{observation}[Omitted Variable Bias in MFRM]\n\\label{obs:ovb}\nLet the full model be $\\eta_{nijk} = \\theta_n - b_i - d_j - \\tau_k + c_n \\cdot e_i$ and the short model omit the contamination term: $\\tilde{\\eta}_{nijk} = \\tilde{\\theta}_n - \\tilde{b}_i - \\tilde{d}_j - \\tau_k$. Under a linear approximation to the ordinal logit link \\citep{wooldridge2010, angrist2009mostly},\\footnote{The OVB formula is exact for linear models. We apply it to the MFRM linear predictor $\\eta$, which is an approximation. Synthetic validation confirms the approximation quality: the Pearson correlation between the predicted bias direction and empirical bias reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), indicating that the linear result accurately characterizes bias direction and relative magnitude.}\n\\begin{equation}\n\\label{eq:ovb}\n\\mathrm{plim}(\\tilde{\\theta}_n - \\theta_n) = \\gamma_n \\cdot c_n,\n\\end{equation}\nwhere $\\gamma_n$ depends on the projection of the exposure vector $(e_i)$ onto the design matrix of the short model (explicit form in Appendix~\\ref{app:gamma}). The bias is non-zero whenever $\\mathrm{Cov}(d_j,\\, c_n \\cdot e_i) \\neq 0$. This captures the confounding component; in the nonlinear MFRM, a specification bias from omitting $c_n \\cdot e_i$ persists even at zero covariance (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{observation}\n\nThe linear approximation in Observation~\\ref{obs:ovb} holds when $c_n \\cdot e_i$ is small relative to the logit scale; for large contamination effects, the gated model (Eq.~\\ref{eq:gmfrm}) captures the full nonlinear interaction without relying on this approximation. The bias combines a specification component from omitting $c_n \\cdot e_i$, which persists regardless of confounding, with a confounding component from non-orthogonality between $d_j$ and $c_n \\cdot e_i$. Monte Carlo simulation confirms that bias magnitude is approximately constant across confounding levels $\\rho \\in \\{0, 0.3, 0.6, 0.9\\}$, indicating that the omission of $c_n \\cdot e_i$---not its correlation with $d_j$---is the primary driver (Figure~\\ref{fig:bias_vs_rho}).\n\nThis confounding undermines both sequential estimation strategies. A bias-first approach, as in CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, calibrates $d_j$ before estimating ability, but the resulting $\\hat{\\theta}$ is already inflated for contaminated models because the omitted $c_n \\cdot e_i$ term has been absorbed into the ability estimates. A contamination-first approach, as in DCR \\citep{dcr2025}, adjusts for contamination before addressing judge effects, but judge severity is then misattributed as contamination signal. Synthetic validation confirms this failure: with $N = 50$ models, $I = 100$ items, $J = 8$ judges, and 20 random seeds, the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$). As illustrated in Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}, the bias is not random but directional: contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig2_ovb_bias}\n\\caption{Sequential estimation bias ($\\hat{\\theta}_{\\mathrm{seq}} - \\theta_{\\mathrm{true}}$) versus true contamination susceptibility $c_n$. Pearson $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$) confirms systematic overestimation of contaminated models. Synthetic data ($N = 50$, $I = 100$, $J = 8$, 20 seeds).}\n\\label{fig:ovb_bias}\n\\end{figure}",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "d433ad07-37ba-44eb-bbe3-18f1e2b49137",

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-prod43/projects/gated_mfrm_judge_bias/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity",
    "newString": "G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity within a measurement-theoretic framework",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nLarge Language Model (LLM) evaluation increasingly relies on LLM-as-a-judge protocols, where one or more LLM judges score model responses on structured benchmarks \\citep{zheng2023mtbench}. Two well-documented sources of distortion affect these evaluations: \\emph{judge bias}, where different judges assign systematically different scores to the same response, and \\emph{benchmark contamination}, where models achieve inflated scores on items encountered during training. Both problems have received sustained research attention, producing separate toolkits for bias calibration and contamination detection. These toolkits treat the two distortions as independent phenomena, a separation that reflects their origins in different research communities rather than any principled argument for independence. Accurate ability ranking is essential for model selection, safety evaluation, and benchmark design; systematic distortion in estimated abilities undermines all three. Yet a practitioner who needs both corrections must apply them sequentially: first correct one source of distortion, then the other. This sequential approach produces unreliable ability estimates. In synthetic experiments with 50 models, 100 items, and 8 judges, sequential estimation yields ability root mean squared error (RMSE) of $0.254 \\pm 0.045$, while joint estimation achieves $0.124 \\pm 0.016$, a 51.2\\% reduction (Wilcoxon $p < 0.001$). The gap does not arise from estimation noise or algorithmic limitations of individual methods; it reflects structural confounding between judge bias and contamination that no sequential pipeline can resolve.\n\nExisting methods address these distortions in isolation. Judge bias corrections---CyclicJudge \\citep{cyclicjudge2026}, UDA \\citep{uda2026}---remove systematic inter-judge differences without modeling contamination. Contamination detection tools---ConStat \\citep{constat2024}, DCR \\citep{dcr2025}, LogProber \\citep{logprober2024}---adjust for memorization without accounting for judge effects (see Section~\\ref{sec:related_work} for detailed review). A bias-first pipeline absorbs contamination-induced score inflation into ability estimates, because the bias model attributes all residual systematic variation to genuine ability. A contamination-first pipeline misattributes judge severity differences as contamination, because score elevation from a lenient judge is indistinguishable from elevation due to memorization without a joint model. In either ordering, the first correction stage produces estimates distorted by the unmodeled facet, and the second stage inherits this distortion.\n\nWe formalize this confounding as an omitted variable bias (OVB) problem \\citep{angrist2009mostly, chernozhukov2022ovb}. Within the Many-Facet Rasch Model (MFRM) framework \\citep{linacre1989mfrm}, the complete data-generating process includes both judge severity $d_j$ and a contamination interaction $c_n \\cdot e_i$. Fitting a specification that omits the contamination term produces biased parameter estimates: the omission of $c_n \\cdot e_i$ induces a specification bias that persists regardless of confounding structure, with an additional directional component when judge severity and contamination are non-orthogonal. The OVB framework predicts that the bias magnitude scales with the contamination susceptibility $c_n$ of each model, so the most contaminated models receive the largest upward distortion in estimated ability. For practical benchmark evaluations, this means that the more a model's training data overlaps with the benchmark, the more its ability is overestimated, precisely the failure mode that contamination correction is designed to prevent. Synthetic validation confirms the predicted pattern: the Pearson correlation between sequential estimation bias and true $c_n$ reaches $r = 0.86$ ($p < 0.001$), which shows that contaminated models are systematically overestimated under both sequential orderings (Section~\\ref{sec:confounding}).\n\nWe propose the Gated Many-Facet Rasch Model (G-MFRM), which combines the multi-facet structure of MFRM \\citep{linacre1989mfrm, jiao2025mfrm_llm} with the gating mechanism of Doubly-Gated Item Response Theory (DG-IRT) \\citep{shu2013dgirt} to jointly estimate model ability, judge severity, and contamination susceptibility in a single measurement framework. G-MFRM extends the MFRM linear predictor with a multiplicative contamination gating term $c_n \\cdot e_i$, where $c_n \\geq 0$ captures model-specific contamination susceptibility and $e_i \\in [0,1]$ quantifies per-item exposure. When $c_n = 0$ or $e_i = 0$, the gating term vanishes and the model reduces to standard MFRM, so uncontaminated model-item pairs incur no distortion. Because the bilinear product $c_n \\cdot e_i$ cannot be uniquely decomposed from response data alone, G-MFRM employs a three-stage calibration procedure: (1) standard MFRM warm-start to obtain initial parameter estimates, (2) external per-item exposure estimation from evidence independent of the response data, and (3) joint Markov chain Monte Carlo (MCMC) sampling with $e_i$ fixed from Stage~2. We provide a formal identifiability analysis (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) that proves fixing $e_i$ externally is both necessary and sufficient for parameter separation under standard multi-judge designs (Section~\\ref{sec:method}). A complementary error propagation analysis bounds the impact of Stage~2 measurement noise: when exposure estimation error satisfies $\\sigma \\leq 0.2$, ability RMSE degradation remains below 6\\%. Figure~\\ref{fig:framework} provides an overview of the complete framework.\n\nWe begin by formalizing the confounding between judge severity and benchmark contamination as omitted variable bias within the MFRM framework (\\S\\ref{sec:confounding}, Figure~\\ref{fig:ovb_bias}), motivating the joint calibration approach. Our contributions are:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item We propose the G-MFRM framework with formal identifiability guarantees (Propositions~\\ref{prop:no_new}--\\ref{prop:exposure_variation} and Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient}) and quantified error propagation bounds for the staged estimation procedure (Section~\\ref{sec:method}, Table~\\ref{tab:error_prop}).\n    \\item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$), with Kendall rank correlation $\\tau = 0.922$ versus $\\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n    \\item On Arena-Hard with 9 LLM judges and 8 target models, G-MFRM produces the first joint diagnostic of per-model contamination susceptibility and per-judge severity, with nominally improved rank agreement ($\\tau = 0.857$ vs.\\ $0.786$ for all seven baselines; Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\\end{enumerate}",

          "     \\item Synthetic validation confirms that joint estimation reduces ability RMSE by 51.2\\% relative to sequential estimation ($p < 0.001$), with Kendall rank correlation $\\tau = 0.922$ versus $\\tau = 0.839$ (Section~\\ref{sec:experiments}).",

  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Non-circularity of exposure estimation.} A potential concern is that exposure estimation is circular if contaminated models inflate the agreement signal defining $e_i$. Three features address this: (1)~$e_i$ is an item-level property (content leakage) while $c_n$ is model-level (susceptibility), so the model tests a cross-level interaction rather than a tautology; (2)~the primary signal (75\\% weight) comes from reference model anchoring against gpt-4-0314, independent of target model scores; (3)~ablation with 100\\% anchoring weight (zero cross-model agreement) yields exposure $\\rho = 0.998$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}), confirming that removing the potentially circular component leaves $e_i$ essentially unchanged.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed"
}

Three features address this: (1)~$e_i$ is an item-level property (content leakage) while $c_n$ is model-level (susceptibility), so the model tests a cross-level interaction rather than a tautology; (2)~the primary signal (75\\% weight) comes from reference model anchoring against gpt-4-0314, independent of target model scores; (3)~ablation with 100\\% anchoring weight (zero cross-model agreement) yields exposure $\\rho = 0.998$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}), confirming that removing the potentially circular component leaves $e_i$ essentially unchanged.\n\n\\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed",
    "originalFile": null,
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 35,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 35,
        "newLines": 8,
        "lines": [
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Stage 2: Exposure estimation.} Per-item exposure $e_i$ is estimated from external evidence independent of the response data used in Stages~1 and~3. The primary signal is \\emph{reference model anchoring}: using a fixed reference model (e.g., gpt-4-0314) as anchor, we compute win-rate residuals for each target model against difficulty-adjusted expectations; items where target models systematically outperform the anchor beyond what difficulty predicts are flagged as potentially exposed. The secondary signal is \\emph{cross-model agreement}: items receiving uniformly high scores across architecturally diverse models suggest exposure rather than genuine easiness, quantified via $z$-score standardization of the inter-model agreement distribution. These signals are combined as a weighted composite (75\\% anchoring, 25\\% agreement), clipped to $[0, 1]$, and treated as fixed in Stage~3. Where model log-probabilities are accessible, LogProber can provide an additional gray-box memorization signal, though it is not used in our current experiments.",
          " ",
          "+\\paragraph{Non-circularity of exposure estimation.} A potential concern is that exposure estimation is circular if contaminated models inflate the agreement signal defining $e_i$. Three features address this: (1)~$e_i$ is an item-level property (content leakage) while $c_n$ is model-level (susceptibility), so the model tests a cross-level interaction rather than a tautology; (2)~the primary signal (75\\% weight) comes from reference model anchoring against gpt-4-0314, independent of target model scores; (3)~ablation with 100\\% anchoring weight (zero cross-model agreement) yields exposure $\\rho = 0.998$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:weight_robustness}), confirming that removing the potentially circular component leaves $e_i$ essentially unchanged.",
          "+",
          " \\paragraph{Stage 3: Joint MCMC.} With $e_i$ fixed, we sample $(\\theta_n, b_i, d_j, c_n)$ jointly via NUTS \\citep{betancourt2015}. The priors are as specified in \\S\\ref{sec:model_spec}. The hierarchical contamination prior decomposes as",
          " %",
          " \\begin{align}"
        ]
      }

    "originalFile": "We identify six limitations. First, although Theorem~\\ref{thm:sufficient} requires only a connected design, the current evaluation achieves 44.7\\% fill rate (5{,}661 of 12{,}672 potential cells); sparser designs may compromise identifiability of $c_n$ for models with few observed exposed items. Relatedly, sparse observations per item produce elevated item-level mean-square residuals (92/176 items), though model- and judge-level fit remains acceptable. Second, Stage~2 exposure estimation relies on a reference model anchor (gpt-4-0314), and exposure estimate quality depends on the anchor remaining uncontaminated relative to the benchmark; as benchmarks age, anchor validity should be periodically re-assessed. Third, the current evaluation uses a single benchmark (Arena-Hard) with eight target models; post-hoc power analysis indicates that detecting $\\Delta\\tau = 0.071$ at $\\alpha = 0.05$ requires $N \\approx 17$ models for 80\\% power, while $N = 8$ yields approximately 50\\% power, explaining the non-significant bootstrap result. Generalization to other benchmarks and larger model populations remains to be verified. Fourth, empirical CI coverage differs by parameter type: ability estimates $\\theta$ approach nominal coverage at moderate sample sizes (${\\sim}$90\\% at $N = 8$), while contamination susceptibility $c_n$ exhibits moderate posterior overconfidence (71\\% at $N = 50$, 68\\% at $N = 8$; \\citealp{talts2018sbc}). The $c_n$ under-coverage reflects the multiplicative coupling $c_n \\cdot e_i$ and the HalfNormal prior's boundary constraint at $c_n = 0$, which systematically narrows posterior credible intervals. Point estimates and rank orderings ($\\tau = 0.857$) are unaffected; $c_n$ estimates are best interpreted as directional diagnostics rather than calibrated intervals. Fifth, while self-evaluation sensitivity analysis (Appendix~\\ref{app:self_eval}) confirms robust rankings when excluding self-judging observations, a more comprehensive analysis removing overlapping models entirely from the judge panel has not been conducted. Sixth, the bilinear contamination term $c_n \\cdot e_i$ assumes rank-1 structure---a single latent dimension of contamination susceptibility per model.
